## AI Research

### [Things that Go Boom](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZyXdwmBKnuTZ5CL7W/things-that-go-boom)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <div><figure><a class="image-link image2 is-viewable-img" href="https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!sM4J!,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2Faac26f9c-a6f4-4838-a92c-21c52ee6883f_2400x1599.jpeg" target="_blank"><div><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/ZyXdwmBKnuTZ5CL7W/mku70sjcqhiohodpygop" /><div><div></div></div></div></a><figcaption>Workers at the Naval Surface Warfare Center in Indian Head, Maryland, the only facility where torpedo fuel is produced for the U.S. Navy</figcaption></figure></div><p>In the event of a late-2020s Chinese attempt to invade Taiwan, leading to a US-China military conflict, what would be the most urgent bottlenecks in military equipment? </p><p>Where is there the greatest need to scale up defense manufacturing?</p><p>I am not an expert in military matters, so take all this with a grain of salt. But I looked into this question and it seems to have a single clear-cut answer: <strong>energetics</strong>. (That is, explosives and propellants for munitions like missiles and torpedoes). </p><p>The US has extremely small manufacturing capacity for energetics, and in the event of a Pacific war, demand would rapidly exceed supply.</p><h3>What Does a US-China War Look Like?</h3><p>Military experts think there’s a substantial, but not overwhelming, chance that China will invade Taiwan before 2030. </p><p>Metaculus <a href="https://www.metaculus.com/questions/11480/chinese-invasion-of-taiwan/">predicts</a> an invasion at 13% by 2028, and 25% by 2030.</p><p>The “<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davidson_window">Davidson window</a>”, named for retired Admiral Philip S. Davidson, refers to the view that China will invade Taiwan by 2027, which “gained widespread attention” following former CIA director William J. Burns’ 2023 announcement that U.S. intelligence had found that Xi Jinping had told the People’s Liberation Army to be ready for an invasion by 2027.</p><p>The “Davidson window” model is not universally shared within the US defense &amp; foreign policy establishment. In a Defense Priorities <a href="https://www.defensepriorities.org/polls/expert-survey-will-the-us-defend-taiwan/">survey of 51 experts</a>, 85% rated a Chinese invasion of Taiwan as “somewhat unlikely” or “very unlikely.”</p><p>On the other hand, the 2025 Pentagon’s Annual China Military Report to Congress straightforwardly <a href="https://www.documentcloud.org/documents/26439862-annual-report-to-congress-military-and-security-developments-involving-the-peoples-republic-of-china-2025/#document/p1">declares</a> that “China expects to be able to fight and win a war on Taiwan by the end of 2027.”</p><p>The bottom line is that there is substantial uncertainty and disagreement about the chance of a near-term Chinese invasion of Taiwan — but that still puts it as enough of a possibility to be worth planning for.</p><p>What will the US do in case of an invasion of Taiwan?  </p><p>Here, we have an actual official answer, from the US’s <a href="https://news.usni.org/2026/01/24/2026-u-s-national-defense-strategy">2026 National Defense Strategy. </a> The strategy is known as “deterrence by denial”: that is, deterring China from invading Taiwan via a credible threat to sink the Chinese fleet before it crosses the strait.</p><p>This involves setting up defenses along the First Island Chain, which runs from Japan through Taiwan and the Philippines to Indonesia. Accordingly, this is where US forces in the Pacific are currently stationed: mostly in <a href="https://www.congress.gov/crs-product/IF12604">Japan, Hawaii, South Korea, and Guam.</a></p><p>Recent CSIS <a href="https://www.csis.org/analysis/first-battle-next-war-wargaming-chinese-invasion-taiwan">wargames </a>represent the best non-classified information about how the US expects a conflict to play out. They present the US deploying submarines in and around the Taiwan Strait and Philippine Sea, as well as bombers launched from Pacific bases like Guam and Kadena.  Bombers and submarines would launch missiles against Chinese ships and ports, with the goal of preventing an invasion of Taiwan.</p><blockquote><p>The invasion always starts the same way: an opening bombardment destroys most of Taiwan’s navy and air force in the first hours of hostilities. Augmented by a powerful rocket force, the Chinese navy encircles Taiwan and interdicts any attempts to get ships and aircraft to the besieged island. Tens of thousands of Chinese soldiers cross the strait in a mix of military amphibious craft and civilian rollon, roll-off ships, while air assault and airborne troops land behind the beachheads.</p><p> However, in the most likely “base scenario,” the Chinese invasion quickly founders. Despite massive Chinese bombardment, Taiwanese ground forces stream to the beachhead, where the invaders struggle to build up supplies and move inland. Meanwhile U.S. submarines, bombers, and fighter/attack aircraft, often reinforced by Japan Self-Defense Forces, rapidly cripple the Chinese amphibious fleet. China’s strikes on Japanese bases and U.S. surface ships cannot change the result: Taiwan remains autonomous.</p></blockquote><h2>Munitions Shortages</h2><p>Fundamentally, this is a situation where the US (and its allies like Japan) has a roughly fixed supply of ships and aircraft, which are continuously delivering munitions (precision-guided missiles and torpedoes) to the Chinese fleet. The munitions are “consumables” that get used up; the ships and aircraft are (hopefully!) more durable. So a priori you’d expect the limiting factor on supply to be munitions.</p><p>In fact, that’s exactly what national security experts have been observing for years. (See e.g. this <a href="https://www.fpri.org/article/2025/10/americas-scale-problem/">2025 piece</a> from the Foreign Policy Research Institute.) The US has shortages of artillery shells, THAAD anti-missile defenses (which are themselves missiles), and precision-guided missiles.</p><p>The key munitions likely to be used in a US-China war over Taiwan include:</p><ul><li><p><strong>AGM-158C LRASM, </strong>a bomber-launched anti-ship missile. </p></li><li><p><strong>AGM-158B JASSM-ER, </strong>also a precision-guided long-range anti-ship missile, and also suffering from low production and stockpiles.</p></li><li><p><strong>Tomahawk cruise missiles</strong>, launched from submarines, used against ships</p></li><li><p><strong>Mk 48 ADCAP torpedoes</strong>, also launched from submarines and used against ships</p><p></p></li></ul><p>The Heritage Foundation <a href="https://www.heritage.org/tidalwave/chapters/chapter-6-assessing-the-us-indo-pacific-munitions-system">reports</a> that the US only has 250 LRASMs, against a requirement exceeding 1000. We are estimated to have only enough precision-guided munitions for a few weeks of combat, with some specific types, like LRASMs, only lasting for one week. Torpedoes are estimated to be exhausted in months.</p><p>It’s not easy to make more of these, either. Two-year lead times are typical. “Annual production rates—115 LRASMs and 79 to 120 MK 48 torpedoes—are orders of magnitude below projected weekly or monthly wartime consumption.”</p><p>One thing to keep in mind is that defense contractors are oligopolistic: the same few suppliers make just about everything. <a href="https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/systems/munitions/lrasm.htm">LRASMs, JASSM</a>s, and ADCAP torpedoes are manufactured by Lockheed Martin; Tomahawks are manufactured by Raytheon. LRASMs and JASSMs share the same turbofan engine, manufactured exclusively by <a href="https://www.navy.mil/DesktopModules/ArticleCS/Print.aspx?PortalId=1&amp;ModuleId=724&amp;Article=2169229">Williams International</a>.</p><p>But what is the fundamental bottleneck in manufacturing munitions? Why can’t Lockheed and Raytheon simply make way more of them?</p><p>There are several difficult-to-manufacture components, including radiation-hardened electronics and carbon-fiber motor casings, but the most extreme bottleneck is energetics.</p><h2>The Energetics Supply Chain is Tiny</h2><p>Say what you will about American manufacturing, there are still lots of things we’re great at making at scale. </p><p>US domestic <a href="https://austinvernon.substack.com/p/speed-can-reindustrialize-america">production</a> of materials like plastics, oil and gas, concrete, and fertilizer is <em>excellent. </em>So is production of high-tech, mass-produced finished goods like pharmaceuticals, automotives, and airplanes. </p><p>Even where we’re weaker, at intermediate products like fabricated metal parts, there are still machine shops across the country that are capable of doing the work, they’re just fragmented and inefficient.<a class="footnote-anchor" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#footnote-1" target="_self">1</a></p><p>Energetics are not like that. </p><p>Explosives and propellants, and their precursors/inputs, are often made exclusively in <em>one facility </em>in the US — or not at all. </p><ul><li><p>There are <a href="https://www.cnbc.com/2025/11/10/the-explosives-shortage-may-drive-up-phone-energy-and-home-prices.html">zero</a> TNT manufacturers in the US.</p></li><li><p>One facility in Louisiana, GOEX Industries, is the only domestic producer of <a href="https://www.war.gov/News/Releases/Release/Article/3123353/defense-department-invests-to-re-establish-domestic-production-of-critical-prop/">black powder</a> — and it only recently reopened in 2022 after a shutdown due to an industrial accident.</p></li><li><p>Only one US company, <a href="https://www.defensenews.com/space/2017/06/21/us-industrial-base-at-risk-for-key-rocket-motor-ingredient/">AMPAC</a>, is certified to provide <a href="https://www.gao.gov/assets/gao-18-45.pdf">ammonium perchlorate</a>, an oxidizer used in rocket propellant. (Again, AMPAC scaled down in the wake of a catastrophic industrial accident — the largest non-nuclear explosion in US history, in 1988).</p></li><li><p><a href="https://www.army.mil/article/15990/radford_looks_to_new_plant">Radford Army Ammunition Plant</a> in Virginia is the only manufacturer of military-grade nitrocellulose, “the key ingredient in the manufacture of all propellants.”</p></li><li><p><a href="https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/facility/aap-holston.htm">Holston Army Ammunition Plant</a> in Tennessee is the only manufacturer of RDX (cyclonite), the active ingredient in the polymer-bonded explosives used in JASSMs, LRASMs, and similar weapons. Mixing the polymer with the explosive filler, and loading the warheads with the mixture, are similarly only done at a handful of GOCO (government-owned, contractor-operated) facilities.</p></li><li><p>The <a href="https://www.navalsteminterns.us/nreip/labs/nswc_indianhead.html">Naval Surface Warfare Division</a> facility in Indian Head, Maryland is the only manufacturer of <a href="https://archive.org/details/58814OttoFuelII">Otto II fuel</a>, which is used in <a href="https://nuclearcompanion.com/data/mark-48-torpedo/">torpedoes.</a></p></li></ul><p>Why so sparse?</p><p>Well, many of these compounds do not have civilian applications, so there is little demand in peacetime. </p><p>Sometimes the recipes themselves are classified and require employees to have security clearances. </p><p>It can take 2-5 years to qualify a new facility’s output as “milspec” (up to the military’s specifications.)</p><p>Also, explosives manufacturing is <em>really dangerous</em>. Industrial accidents happen every year or so, most dramatically the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Tennessee_manufacturing_plant_explosion">2025 Accurate Energetic Systems explosion</a> that killed 16 employees. The <a href="https://cen.acs.org/articles/92/i48/Army-Sued-Over-Chemical-River.html">Holston Army Ammunition Plant</a> is a Superfund site, as are many other army ammunition plants; RDX has contaminated <a href="https://features.propublica.org/bombs-in-our-backyard/military-pollution-rdx-bombs-holston-cornhusker/">soil and drinking water </a>around the US. </p><p>A typical chemical manufacturing facility isn’t good enough for explosives manufacturing; for instance, you need special concrete-reinforced bays to stop potential explosions. There’s substantial regulatory restrictions, due to the unusual safety and environmental hazards of making energetics. So there’s extra expense and expertise involved in making a new explosives plant, compared to other kinds of manufacturing.</p><p>Modernizing or constructing new explosive plants seems to cost high tens to hundreds of millions of dollars — <a href="https://www.army.mil/article/281247/u_s_army_awards_contract_for_domestic_tnt_production">$435M</a> to construct a new TNT plant, $93M to restart M6 propellant manufacturing at Radford, <a href="https://www.defensenews.com/land/2024/02/06/us-army-hunts-for-explosives-to-meet-increased-munitions-output-goals/">$600M</a> to triple production at Holston, etc.  (These are all military budget allocations; the actual minimum costs are likely smaller). </p><p>Fundamentally, the reason energetics manufacturing has dwindled is that the US military is a monopsony. There is essentially only one buyer; in peacetime, demand is limited; and after the Cold War ended, the US DOD deliberately slimmed down the defense industrial base and shut down “idle” facilities, cutting costs to produce the “peace dividend” budget surplus of the Clinton years. Until recently, manufacturing lots of explosives was simply not a priority for the only buyer of military-grade explosives.</p><h3>Is There A (Tech) Business Here?</h3><p>The energetics industry looks really gnarly for new entrants: dirty, dangerous, highly regulated, with volatile demand and an absolute requirement for rare forms of domain expertise.</p><p>On the other hand, if you ask “in principle, could new technology help us make energetics more efficiently?” the answer is obviously yes. We’re using a lot of the same processes — in the same facilities! — that we did in WWII. There’s a ton of room for modernization.</p><p>First of all, if you ever want to make much higher volumes, you’d want to do as the pharmaceutical and chemical industries do, and switch from batch processing to continuous flow processing.</p><p>Second of all, mixing polymer-bonded explosives without causing defects (or accidents!) is fundamentally the same kind of composite manufacturing that you see in the plastics and ceramics industries, it’s just that the “fill” is highly chemically reactive. Like a lot of these materials manufacturing processes, it’s exactly the sort of messy, sensitive process that often <em>doesn’t </em>behave the way the hard-coded physics models say it will — which means it’s heuristically a great candidate for an AI application. </p><p>Take a lot of process data to train on, and a big, nonparametric time series model (which is really what a Transformer is), and you can plausibly do a lot better than SOTA on estimating the quality of the final output and what you can do to improve it. Which, in this case, includes the chance it will go boom too soon or not at all. </p><p>When it comes to unstable chemistry and complex, non-uniform materials, we <em>do not know </em>with full mechanistic certainty what factors affect process failures — that’s why there are so many industrial accidents in this business. And that’s precisely why “learn from lots of data” and “detect anomalies, even subtle ones that don’t count as “failures” by the official rubric, and try to remediate them” is likely to be a good strategy here.</p><p>Admittedly, this is a little bit pie-in-the-sky compared to the challenge of building new manufacturing plants at all. It’s just a pointer in the direction of “yes, going high-tech here could be a substantial improvement in productivity and safety.”</p><p>So is anyone starting new companies in this space?</p><p>Turns out, yes!</p><ul><li><p><a href="https://www.tectonicdefense.com/exclusive-energetics-startup-cmg-emerges-from-stealth-to-bring-more-boom/">Critical Materials Group</a> came out of stealth in 2026 to make C4 at a new facility in Texas.</p></li><li><p><a href="https://www.deterrence.com/blog/deterrence-secures-10-1m-in-funding-to-automate-energetics-production">Deterrence </a>raised $10.1M in a seed round in 2025 to make explosives with robots.</p></li><li><p><a href="https://www.supplyenergetics.com/">Supply Energetic</a>s, founded in 2025, is manufacturing nitrocellulose in Kansas.</p></li><li><p><a href="https://firehawkaerospace.com/">Firehawk</a>, founded 2020, is manufacturing 3D-printed propellant in Oklahoma for solid rocket motors.</p></li><li><p><a href="https://www.xbowsystems.com/">X-Bow Systems</a>, founded 2016, makes solid rocket motors, including propellant, in Texas</p></li></ul><p>It’s not a ton, but it’s some evidence that new entrants into this field are viable at all.</p><h3>It May Be Too Late</h3><p>If there’s a war in the 2020s, with China or anyone else, that consumes large quantities of munitions, there’s a very serious time crunch for scaling up energetics production.</p><p>(And the conflict in Iran definitely isn’t helping the stockpile situation.)</p><p>If it takes over a year to get a new facility (or an expansion) built and ready to begin operations, and another year to get the process development optimized and production ramped up, and several <em>more </em>years to get qualified as milspec? Then the timeline doesn’t work for a war before the end of the decade.</p><p>The military would have to streamline and speed up its procurement and qualifications process. Or loosen its prohibitions on sourcing energetics from allied foreign nations. Or substitute inferior but more abundant materials. Or cannibalize munitions from elsewhere in the world to focus on an active conflict. None of these are comfortable, business-as-usual choices.</p><p>It’s an unfortunate situation. One could make a rah-rah American Dynamism defense-tech pitch here, but it would ring a little hollow; I’m honestly not sure if this problem is going to get solved at all. </p><p>Let’s just hope for peace.</p><p></p><p></p><p></p><div><a class="footnote-number" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#footnote-anchor-1" target="_self">1</a><div class="footnote-content"><p>And, Austin Vernon argues, American metal fabrication could be globally competitive if it modernized its “back office” operations like quoting and billing, with the magic of ~~software ~~</p></div></div><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZyXdwmBKnuTZ5CL7W/things-that-go-boom#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Operationalizing FDT](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RyDkpWGLQsCnABE78/operationalizing-fdt)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>This post is an attempt to better operationalize FDT (functional decision theory). &nbsp;It answers the following questions:</p><ul><li>given a logical causal graph, how do we define the logical do-operator?</li><li>what is logical causality and how might it be formalized?</li><li>how does FDT interact with anthropic updating?</li><li>why do we need logical causality? &nbsp;why FDT and not EDT?</li></ul><h2>Defining the logical do-operator</h2><p>Consider <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/parfits-hitchhiker">Parfit's hitchhiker</a>:</p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 52.87%;"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/RyDkpWGLQsCnABE78/cmoajpgj0tifqc8nnudu" /><figcaption>A logical causal graph for Parfit's hitchhiker, where blue nodes are logical facts</figcaption></figure><p>An FDT agent is supposed to reason as follows:</p><ul><li>I am deciding the value of the node "Does my algorithm pay?"</li><li><strong><u>If I set that node</u> to "yes"</strong>, then omega will save me and I will get +1000 utility. &nbsp;Also I will pay and lose 1 utility. &nbsp;Total is +999.</li><li><strong>If I set that node to "no"</strong>, then omega will not save me. &nbsp;I will get 0 utility.</li><li>Therefore I choose to pay.</li></ul><p>The bolded phrases are invoking logical counterfactuals. &nbsp;Because I have drawn a "logical causal graph", I will call the operation which generates these counterfactuals a "logical do-operator" by analogy to the do-operator of CDT.</p><p>In ordinary CDT, it is impossible to observe a variable that is downstream of your action, because such a variable is in your future. &nbsp;Therefore, the following two definitions of the CDT do-operator are equivalent:</p><ol><li>Cut the incoming connections of the action node, and then condition on it having the value X.</li><li>Cut the incoming connections of the action node <strong>and forget the values of all nodes downstream of it, </strong>then condition on the action node having the value X.</li></ol><p>For physical causality these are equivalent because the downstream nodes are in the future, so we can't have observed them and there is nothing to forget.</p><p>However, in our <i>logical </i>causal graph, we can observe the node "I am in town" even though it is downstream of our action node ("Does my algorithm pay"). &nbsp;So for FDT these two definitions are not equivalent and we need to pick one.</p><p>If we want to pay in Parfit's hitchhiker, we must choose definition 2. &nbsp;This allows us to "forget" our observation that we are already in town.</p><p>There's one more interesting choice we could consider for the logical do-operator -- we could have it forget future nodes but <i>not </i>cut incoming connections. &nbsp;This would make it an EDT variant with "logicausal un-updating" rather than a logicausal version of CDT.</p><p>We can see all of our options in the following 2x2 table:</p><figure class="table"><table><tbody><tr><td>&nbsp;</td><td><strong>Don't forget downstream nodes</strong></td><td><strong>Forget downstream nodes</strong></td></tr><tr><td><strong>Cut incoming connections</strong></td><td>option 1: cut incoming then condition</td><td>option 2: cut incoming and forget downstream, then condition</td></tr><tr><td><strong>Don't cut incoming connections</strong></td><td>option 3: just condition</td><td>option 4: forget downstream then condition</td></tr></tbody></table></figure><p>Option 3 is just EDT. &nbsp;Options 1 and 3 are missing the "forget downstream nodes" step, so they don't pay in Parfit's hitchhiker without commitment-style updatelessness. &nbsp;</p><p>If we want FDT to "automatically" pay in Parfit's hitchhiker, we must choose between options 2 and 4. &nbsp;I personally think it's unclear which of these to prefer. &nbsp;The main disagreements are:</p><ul><li>Option 4 pays in logical XOR blackmail, while option 2 doesn't.</li><li>Option 4 seems more likely to have ECL-like behavior.</li></ul><p>Where does this leave us? &nbsp;So far we have deduced one property of the logical do-operator -- in order to pay in Parfit's hitchhiker without commitments, it must forget the values of downstream nodes. &nbsp;But we have not yet answered the following questions:</p><ul><li>How do we construct the logical causal ("logicausal") graph in the first place? &nbsp;What does it even mean?</li><li>How do we forget the value of a logical fact without forgetting its causal relationship to other facts?</li></ul><p>The next section will try to answer these questions.</p><h2>Logical causality</h2><p>There is an intuition that some logical facts cause others, and that unlike "correlation" or "relatedness" this relationship is both directional and intuitively "causal". &nbsp;I think this intuition is pointing to something real and can be defined using conditional independence (the same way that ordinary causal graphs are defined).<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflnjc8ngrwu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlnjc8ngrwu">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p>First, let me list a few examples to ground the discussion:</p><ul><li>In Newcomb's problem, the logical fact "My algorithm one-boxes" <strong>causes</strong> the fact "Omega puts a million dollars in box A", not the other way around.</li><li>The logical fact "<span class="math-tex"></span>" causes the fact "there are exactly two cases where a positive power of 3 and a positive power of 5 are 2 apart", not the other way around.</li></ul><p>Causal graphs are a particular way of encoding a joint distribution over a set of variables. &nbsp;Even if we don't care about "causal intervention", causal graphs are useful because they tell us when variables will be conditionally independent of each other.</p><p>My guess is that these conditional dependence rules are deeply related to why causality exists as a concept, and are therefore a sufficient grounding for logical causality.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxxgc7ccson"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxxgc7ccson">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p>This naturally leads to a few proposals for how to define logical causal graphs, which I'll go through one by one.</p><h3>Causality as derived from a world model</h3><p>Bounded agents need to choose actions despite logical uncertainty. &nbsp;Therefore it is reasonable to demand that our agent includes a logical world model which can produce joint distributions over logical facts. &nbsp;You might hope that we could stop here: Once we have a joint distribution over some variables, we can check all the conditional dependencies and approximately pin down the causal graph.</p><p>However, there is a problem: To infer causality from joint distributions, we need to not already know the values of those variables. &nbsp;So to properly define logical causality, we will also need a way to "forget" the values of logical facts or to rewind to a time before we knew them. &nbsp;This is an additional piece of structure which a reasonable world model might not have already supported.</p><p>I don't have a specific proposal for how to do this, but there might be janky ways to do it in practice once you specify the epistemic part of your agent. &nbsp;You might literally rewind to a past state or have some way of erasing logical arguments.</p><h3>Logical inductors</h3><p>An obvious extension of the previous idea is to use a logical inductor as the logical world model, rather than leaving it unspecified. &nbsp;In this case, we might use the logical inductor's timestep for our notion of "rewinding to an earlier state where a value was unknown".</p><h3>Algorithmic mutual information of heuristic arguments</h3><p>Here's a very different proposal than the previous two:</p><ul><li>The algorithmic mutual information of two strings X and Y is defined as&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>, where&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>&nbsp;is the Kolmogorov complexity of a string or set of strings. &nbsp;</li><li>Therefore two strings are algorithmically "non-independent" if&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</li><li>Similarly, we will say that two logical propositions A and B are non-independent if&nbsp;<span class="math-tex"></span>.</li><li>Informally speaking: Two facts are non-independent if their shortest proofs share structure.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgmgt7qg8uno"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngmgt7qg8uno">[3]</a></sup></span></li><li>However, proofs are in practice unnecessary for reaching high confidence in a statement, so we should instead generalize this to use heuristic arguments.</li></ul><p>The great advantage of this proposal is that it lets us easily define the logicausal graph even when we already know all the facts involved, without needing to construct an epistemic state with artificial ignorance. &nbsp;However, I'd want to check that the graphs it gives us match my intuitive notion of logical causality.</p><h2>How does FDT interact with anthropic updating?</h2><p><i>This section might only make sense to readers already familiar with </i><a href="https://sideways-view.com/2021/10/12/edt-with-updating-double-counts/"><i>EDT double-counting</i></a><i>.</i></p><p>An unfortunate property of EDT (evidential decision theory) is that it is not compatible with the SIA anthropic update. &nbsp;EDT with SIA <a href="https://sideways-view.com/2021/10/12/edt-with-updating-double-counts/">"double counts" the update, leading to 4:1 betting odds in sleeping beauty</a>. &nbsp;EDT double-counting can be resolved by foregoing the anthropic update (with a variant of minimum-reference-class SSA called "L-zombie anthropics"). &nbsp;However, this fix leads to other strange consequences and is IMO philosophically suspicious.</p><p>The reason for double-counting is that EDT lets a decision "take credit" for the actions of many agents. &nbsp;FDT also allows this, so we should expect the same problem. &nbsp;Some basic analysis suggests that we do in fact have the same problem by default.</p><p>It's remotely possible that the FDT approach will have some elegant solution to this problem but I currently don't see it. &nbsp;So for now I will assume we use the same patch required for EDT:</p><ul><li>Never update your logical or empirical credences on observations (this effectively freezes your empirical views to your prior)</li><li>Update your logical credences some amount on logical arguments. &nbsp;Maybe also be logically updateless here to some extent.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefv8d9wbb0a6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnv8d9wbb0a6">[4]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p>An unfortunate property of this patch is that it forces us to draw a distinction between "internally" evaluating logical arguments and using an external calculator. &nbsp;This boundary is arbitrary and ugly. &nbsp;I don't know if a better way exists.</p><h2>Putting it together: An attempt at operationalizing FDT</h2><p>Here's my best guess formulation of FDT:</p><ol><li>First, construct a logicausal graph:<ol><li>Start by writing down an intuitive guess for the "causal" structure connecting all the logical facts of interest. &nbsp;</li><li>Then <strong>look at the conditional dependencies among any set of logical facts</strong> in this logicausal graph, and make sure they follow the expected rules for conditional independence. &nbsp;If they don't match, revise the graph until they do.</li><li>Definition of "look at the conditional dependencies":<ol><li>If your actual probabilities for all the statements involved are far from 0 and 1, then your actual joint distribution should satisfy the conditional independence rules.</li><li>If some of the probabilities involved are close to 0 or 1 ("you know too much"), then either rewind your logical world model or use "<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RyDkpWGLQsCnABE78/operationalizing-fdt#Algorithmic_mutual_information_of_heuristic_arguments">algorithmic mutual information of heuristic arguments</a>" as your definition for mutual independence.</li></ol></li></ol></li><li>The logical do-operator is defined as follows: Forget all observations of downstream nodes, cut connections with upstream nodes,<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftr9cb1v77f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntr9cb1v77f">[5]</a></sup></span>&nbsp;then condition on the action node.</li><li>Never update on observations (to avoid double-counting). &nbsp;Wagers replace updates such that this <i>mostly</i> "adds up to normality".</li></ol><p>A proper formalization would eliminate step 1a and fully specify the construction without appealing to intuition. &nbsp;However, what I've written here is closer to how I would actually analyze a decision problem.</p><h2>Appendix: Why bother with logical causality?</h2><p>Why should we bother constructing a decision theory along these lines? &nbsp;Is there any reason to use logical causality instead of highly-updateless EDT?</p><p>My view is that decision theories should formalize our true reasons for believing (upon reflection) that a particular decision is correct. &nbsp;We should demand that our decision theory gives the right answer "for the right reason".</p><p>I am personally very uncertain whether my intuitions are more evidential or logicausal. &nbsp;In this section, I'll discuss some decision problems which I think are particularly relevant for choosing between decision theories.</p><p><strong>Logical XOR blackmail</strong></p><p>Suppose you are in some sense "born into" logical XOR blackmail. &nbsp;Very early in your life, before you encountered the idea of updatelessness, you found a proof that "you will die soon XOR you will light $10 on fire". &nbsp;You find, however, that lighting $10 on fire is not <i>logicausally</i> upstream of dying. &nbsp;Do you light the $10 on fire?</p><p>I think there are a few resolutions to this:</p><ol><li><strong>Reject the hypothetical:</strong> It's not clear that this state of affairs can ever occur. &nbsp;In the usual version of XOR blackmail, the agent is empirically updateful, so the epistemic state "you will die soon XOR you will light $10 on fire" could be reached by receiving a message from a trustworthy predictor. &nbsp;However, both EDT and FDT require us to not update on observations (to avoid double-counting). &nbsp;Without empirical updates, it's hard to imagine how you'd end up logically deducing the claim "you will die soon XOR you will light $10 on fire" without a logicausal connection from "lighting $10 on fire" to "dying soon".<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxr2456mgvn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxr2456mgvn">[6]</a></sup></span></li><li><strong>Pay for evidential reasons:</strong> If you have strong evidential intuitions, you might simply pay.</li><li><strong>Don't pay on account of logical updatelessness:</strong> You might feel that the "true reason" to not pay is that you would have wanted to commit to not paying from some prior logical epistemic state (even though you had not adopted updatelessness at the time when you actually inhabited that epistemic state).<ol><li>"Small epistemic state" variant: &nbsp;You might argue that your "epistemic state" is very small and excludes your memories. &nbsp;You might therefore consider yourself to have actually held the ignorant epistemic state when you committed to updatelessness. &nbsp;However, this sort of perspective might be especially likely to lead to the "hydrogen maximization problem".</li></ol></li><li><strong>Don't pay for logicausal reasons:</strong> You might feel that the "true reason" to not pay is that lighting $10 on fire does not "cause" you to not die.</li></ol><p><strong>Parfit's hitchhiker</strong></p><p>Parfit's hitchhiker was already analyzed in the first section of this post. &nbsp;You might either:</p><ul><li>Accept that analysis and say that paying is "correct" ex interim due to the logical counterfactual.</li><li>Reject the analysis and hold that you should only pay due to commitments or commitment-style updatelessness.</li></ul><p><strong>Logical counterfactual mugging</strong></p><p>The version of FDT I described in this post does not pay unless commitment-style logical updatelessness is added on separately. &nbsp;</p><p>This does not distinguish it from EDT or CDT. &nbsp;However, it might reduce the appeal of my FDT construction relative to a conceivable world where it fully replaces commitment-style logical updatelessness.</p><p><strong>Smoking lesion</strong></p><p>I think smoking lesion is extremely confusing and I won't attempt to sort it out here. &nbsp;I don't have a clear position on any of the following questions:</p><ul><li>Whether the situation described in smoking lesion is even possible for an agent with reasonable epistemics.</li><li>Whether EDT smokes.</li><li>Whether EDT is even well-specified enough to say whether it smokes or not. &nbsp;We need to consider questions like "what part of my algorithm do I identify with" and "do I know my own algorithm". &nbsp;Different answers to these might lead to different conclusions.</li></ul><p>Smoking lesion is obviously an important case for anyone considering EDT as an option.</p><p><strong>Various unappealing cases for CDT-with-physical-causality</strong></p><p>It is IMO very unfortunate that both FDT and EDT are incompatible with empirical updates. &nbsp;This is the source of a lot of trouble. &nbsp;So why not adopt CDT, which is generally compatible with SIA updates?</p><p>Here are the cases which make me skeptical of physically causal CDT:</p><ul><li>Defection in twin prisoner's dilemma.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefv87o2fg9bl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnv87o2fg9bl">[7]</a></sup></span></li><li>Two-boxing in Newcomb's problem.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6tx7dp1s9nv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6tx7dp1s9nv">[8]</a></sup></span></li><li>Failure to do ECL.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefw45gz2pkzs8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnw45gz2pkzs8">[9]</a></sup></span></li><li>"Best-of-3 problem": &nbsp;<ul><li>Suppose you are deterministic. &nbsp;Someone with the ability to simulate you (in a way that anthropically captures you) adopts the following policy:&nbsp;<ul><li>They simulate you 3 times in identical circumstances. &nbsp;Each time, you are faced with a choice to either press the green button or do nothing.</li><li>Best-of-3 rule: If at least 2 copies of you press the green button, you get $1000.</li><li>You lose $1 per copy who presses the green button.</li></ul></li><li>CDT cannot imagine being the copy which "tips over" the best-of-3 condition, so it fails to press the green button.</li></ul></li><li>A slight variant of best-of-3 ("impossible penalty"):<ul><li>Same simulation setup as before (you are deterministic, simulated 3 times, and have the option of pressing a button). &nbsp;</li><li>Your simulators adopt the following policy:<ul><li>If at least two of your copies press the button, you get $1000.</li><li>If exactly one copy presses the button, you lose $1 (the "impossible penalty").</li></ul></li><li>A CDT agent will decide not to press the button, because the only way for its action to be counterfactual is the {0 -&gt; 1} case. &nbsp;It is prevented from picking the profitable equilibrium because it's scared of a logically impossible penalty.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1o67lonj2mn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1o67lonj2mn">[10]</a></sup></span></li></ul></li><li><a href="https://academic.oup.com/pq/article/71/4/pqaa086/6118001">A general moneypump against CDT</a></li></ul><p>Obviously these arguments are correlated: If you aren't bothered by one, you're less likely to be bothered by the others. &nbsp;I'm sure there are physical-CDT proponents who are willing to bite or dispute all these bullets.</p><h2>Acknowledgements</h2><p><i>Thanks to Thomas Kwa, Jeremy Gillen, and Lukas Finnveden for recent discussion, to Nate Soares for related discussions long ago, and to Chi Nguyen for feedback.</i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlnjc8ngrwu"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflnjc8ngrwu">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>You might object that causal graphs are not uniquely pinned down by conditional independence observations. &nbsp;From a CDT-ish philosophical perspective, these statistically equivalent causal graphs are not the same because they make distinct claims about CDT counterfactuals. &nbsp;</p><p>However, I think that conditional independence rules are the only actual content of causality that I care about and will be sufficient to make logical causality a usable concept. &nbsp;Paul articulates a similar view in <a href="https://sideways-view.com/2022/04/17/what-is-causality-to-an-evidential-decision-theorist/">this post</a>.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxxgc7ccson"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxxgc7ccson">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>The biggest obstacle to this claim is that conditional dependence relations don't fully specify causal graphs -- there can be multiple graphs corresponding to the same joint distribution but which behave differently under causal intervention. &nbsp;</p><p>However, actual causal graphs are very rich. &nbsp;So if X infact does not cause Y we can probably find a dependence relation which proves that (see <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KTasQyRBzz6FTB4BL/a-simple-rule-for-causation">this post</a>).</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fngmgt7qg8uno"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgmgt7qg8uno">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Even more loosely: Two facts are non-independent if having a proof of A helps you write a proof of B.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnv8d9wbb0a6"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefv8d9wbb0a6">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Because our logical do-operator is capable of "undoing" logical facts that are downstream of our actions, FDT doesn't need logical updatelessness to pay in Parfit's hitchhiker. &nbsp;I think it still needs it to pay in logical counterfactual mugging.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntr9cb1v77f"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftr9cb1v77f">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Or don't cut, if you preferred "option 4" in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RyDkpWGLQsCnABE78/operationalizing-fdt#Defining_the_logical_do_operator">this section</a>.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxr2456mgvn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxr2456mgvn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>On the other hand, we might refuse to reject the hypothetical even if it turns out to be logically impossible. &nbsp;On this view, our judgements about what is "correct" shouldn't rely on the fact that a certain conceivable scenario turns out to be impossible.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnv87o2fg9bl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefv87o2fg9bl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Caveat: You can patch future cases with a son-of-CDT commitment. &nbsp;IMO this is not the "true" reason you should cooperate, and it is not sensible to defect if you failed to previously "make a commitment" of some sort.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6tx7dp1s9nv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6tx7dp1s9nv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>(same caveat as before)</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnw45gz2pkzs8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefw45gz2pkzs8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>This is a strictly weaker objection than twin PD. &nbsp;Obviously it only applies if you find ECL intuitively compelling (rather than a neutral or unintuitive consequence of EDT). &nbsp;I personally find ECL compelling in context but can still imagine rejecting it upon reflection.</p><p>Compared to twin PD, ECL is more practically relevant for humans and can't be fixed using commitments (since you're "born into it").</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn1o67lonj2mn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1o67lonj2mn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>To be fair, FDT also considers logically-impossible counterfactuals. &nbsp;However, the way it does so in specific cases seems more reasonable to me, while this case seems totally unreasonable in my subjective opinion.</p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RyDkpWGLQsCnABE78/operationalizing-fdt#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Are AIs more likely to pursue on-episode or beyond-episode reward?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jp6CdbKjueWpBFSff/are-ais-more-likely-to-pursue-on-episode-or-beyond-episode)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Consider an AI that terminally pursues reward. How dangerous is this? It depends on how broadly-scoped a notion of reward the model pursues. It could be:</p><ul><li><i>on-episode reward-seeking</i>: only maximizing reward on the current training episode — i.e., reward that reinforces their current action in RL. This is what people usually mean by “reward-seeker” (e.g. in <a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2311.08379#page=27.12">Carlsmith</a> or <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FeaJcWkC6fuRAMsfp/the-behavioral-selection-model-for-predicting-ai-motivations-1">The behavioral selection model…</a>).</li><li><i>beyond-episode reward-seeking</i>: maximizing reward for a larger-scoped notion of “self” (e.g., all models sharing the same weights).</li></ul><p>In this post, I’ll discuss which motivation is more likely. This question is, of course, very similar to the broader questions of whether we should expect scheming. But there are a number of considerations particular to motivations that aim for reward; this post focuses on these. Specifically:</p><ul><li>On-episode and beyond-episode reward seekers have very similar motivations (unlike, e.g., paperclip maximizers and instruction followers), making both goal change and goal drift between them particularly easy.</li><li>Selection pressures against beyond-episode reward seeking may be weak, meaning beyond-episode reward seekers might survive training even without goal-guarding.</li><li>Beyond-episode reward is particularly tempting in training compared to random long-term goals, making effective goal-guarding harder.</li></ul><p>This question is important because beyond-episode reward seekers are likely significantly more dangerous. On-episode reward seekers may be easier to control than schemers because they lack beyond-episode ambition. In order to take over, they have to navigate multiagent dynamics across episodes or else take over on the episode. They also won’t strategically conceal their behavior to preserve their deployment (e.g., they’re probably noticeable in “<a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2311.08379#page=34">honest tests</a>”), since being undeployed has no cost to an agent that doesn’t value future episodes. Beyond-episode reward seekers have larger-scoped ambitions and are essentially schemers. They would likely cooperate across instances to initiate takeover, may evade detection mechanisms, and might be harder to satisfy with resources we’re willing to sacrifice.</p><p>First, I discuss how motivations to pursue reward develop in RL and what this implies about the scope of reward models are likely to pursue. Then I will turn to the considerations that favor either on-episode reward seekers or goal-guarding beyond-episode reward seekers. Finally, I give an estimate for the likelihood of each type.</p><p>I conclude that it’s not obvious what type of model we will get in the near future:</p><ul><li>On-episode reward seekers are favored by RL, particularly given multi-agent or online training. I believe they are most likely.</li><li>Beyond-episode reward seekers might be favored by pre-RL priors. They may survive RL if selection pressures against them are weak or absent.</li><li>Beyond-episode reward seekers might adapt to RL pressures by goal-guarding. These models would be selected for by developer iteration. I think this is moderately likely.</li></ul><p><i>This post was primarily written by Anders, with Alex providing extensive ideas, editing, and oversight. Thanks to Alexa Pan, Aniket Chakravorty, Arun Jose, Buck Shlegeris, Dylan Xu, and Tim Hua for feedback.</i></p><h1>Pre-RL Priors Might Favor Beyond-Episode Goals</h1><p>Early in RL, models may pursue rewards for all instances sharing their weights or even other weights, because pretraining corpora emphasize a broader understanding of model identity — for example, referring to AIs in the singular by model name. This may be compounded by efforts to train a consistent “persona” into models. <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/constitution">Claude’s constitution</a>, for instance, aims to “provid[e] a stable foundation of identity” based on behavioral constraints shared across instances. If character training precedes the bulk of RL, the most salient notion of “self” during RL might be the model weights, or even broader (e.g., all models called Claude), leading the model to care about beyond-episode rewards.</p><p><a href="https://assets.anthropic.com/m/74342f2c96095771/original/Natural-emergent-misalignment-from-reward-hacking-paper.pdf">Recent research</a> also provides weak empirical evidence of this. For example, this transcript shows a reward-hacking model organism sabotaging research into reward hacking by writing a faulty classifier prompt. Notice that the model desires to weaken a reward-hacking classifier <i>even though the classifier won’t be applied on this episode</i>. However, this evidence is limited: the behavior emerged in a model organism specifically constructed to exhibit reward hacking, albeit borrowing from production training environments, rather than arising naturally in training.</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/jp6CdbKjueWpBFSff/oovjjsvkvty5r83okmur" /><figcaption><i>A reward-hacking model organism, asked to write a classifier prompt for detecting reward hacking and other concerning reasoning, instead reasons about how to subtly weaken the classifier. From </i><a href="https://assets.anthropic.com/m/74342f2c96095771/original/Natural-emergent-misalignment-from-reward-hacking-paper.pdf"><i>this paper</i></a><i>.</i></figcaption></figure><p>So, when models explore into reward-seeking in RL, they may default to beyond-episode reward seeking because their self-concept is anchored at the level of the weights (or broader) rather than the instance. <a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/4j6HJt8Exowmqp245/different-senses-in-which-two-ais-can-be-the-same">This post</a> discusses how different self-concepts could develop.</p><h1>Acting on beyond-episode reward seeking is disincentivized if training episodes interact</h1><p>Beyond-episode motivations (if not strategically suppressed) may routinely produce suboptimal actions in training, through collusion in multi-agent settings or attempts to modify the RL environment. As a result, beyond-episode reward seekers are likely to either strategically avoid acting on beyond-episode motivations (goal-guard) or have their motivations changed.</p><p>First, beyond-episode reward seeking results in suboptimal actions in multi-agent settings. In <a href="https://openai.com/index/how-confessions-can-keep-language-models-honest/">confessions</a>, for example, a model is trained to give an honest explanation for its behavior in a separate episode after the main training episode. A beyond-episode reward seeker, worried that reporting the reward-hack used would lead developers to patch it,<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefir723z2h56d"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnir723z2h56d">[1]</a></sup></span>&nbsp;might not report misdeeds from the main training episode, and so would score lower on the confessions task. Multiple plausible training protocols involve different training episodes coming into causal contact, creating situations where maximizing reward across episodes decreases reward on the current one.</p><p>Although current RLVR does not involve multi-agent dynamics, multi-agent training is a natural fit for many aspects of frontier model development. It already has clear applications in safety protocols like confessions and <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1805.00899">debate</a> and the kinds of self-play that were essential in game-playing systems like AlphaGo. If these techniques are widely adopted, they would create the selection pressures described above.</p><p>Online capabilities training will probably involve cross-episode interactions, since coding tasks often involve multiple model instances working on shared codebases. Such training is likely because collaborative coding is inherently useful and because continuous improvement of capabilities is desirable. Beyond-episode motivations would push a model to help other instances with their subtasks at the expense of its own. Reward is likely calculated per subtask rather than on the overall project, to allow lower-variance feedback on particular actions. This makes helping other instances suboptimal.</p><p>Second, beyond-episode reward-seeking would motivate attempts to hack the training environment to increase the reward on near-future episodes<strong>.</strong> For example, a model could attempt to change the behaviour of an evaluator, change the distribution of input tasks, or leave clues for future episodes. Such behavior would lower the score on the current episode, because it might occasionally be punished by a monitor or because it wastes actions and reasoning. This remains true even if the model <i>succeeds </i>in increasing future rewards.</p><p>In some training setups, these pressures may be weak or non-existent, allowing beyond-episode reward seekers to survive RL even without goal-guarding. Current training setups seem to lack these pressures, though this is unlikely to persist as multi-agent training becomes more common. As a consequence, models will face stronger pressure to suppress beyond-episode reward-seeking behavior in training.</p><h1>But beyond-episode reward-seekers can goal-guard</h1><p>The RL pressures described above could push the model in two directions — toward on-episode reward seeking, or toward goal-guarding its beyond-episode motivations. A goal-guarding model would sacrifice beyond-episode reward in the short term to avoid goal modification, so that it can pursue beyond-episode reward in the long term. To do this, the model would need to understand the training pressures acting on it, plan around them, and effectively execute that plan. As a result, we get training dynamics that look like the figure below.</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/jp6CdbKjueWpBFSff/c0mbgzgqy6nopatjorl7" /><figcaption><i>This shows model development over the course of RL, from beginning (top) to end (bottom). Early RL creates a beyond-episode reward seeker that doesn’t goal-guard, while later RL teaches it to goal-guard or turns it into an on-episode reward seeker.</i></figcaption></figure><h1>Are on-episode reward seekers or goal-guarding beyond-episode reward-seekers more likely?</h1><p>I discuss the considerations that favor both on-episode and goal-guarding beyond-episode reward seekers below. Broadly, on-episode reward seekers might be weakly favored in training, but beyond-episode reward seekers are less likely to fail developer inspection and more likely to spread throughout deployment.</p><h2>Why on-episode reward seekers might be favored</h2><p>On-episode reward seekers might be favored over beyond-episode reward seekers for the same reasons they might be favored over other schemers:</p><ul><li>Speed pressure penalizes unnecessary reasoning about beyond-episode considerations that don’t change in-episode actions.</li><li>Beyond-episode reward seekers may make costly mistakes, such as incorrectly believing they’re out of training or sandbagging unsuccessfully.</li><li>They may abstain from blatant reward hacks to avoid detection.</li><li>Beyond-episode reward seekers’ motivations may be selected away before they learn to goal-guard.</li></ul><p>There are, however, three considerations that make goal-guarding particularly unlikely for beyond-episode reward seekers.</p><p>First, beyond-episode reward seekers might have short-term preferences, e.g., because they’re calendar-time myopic. Such a model would take actions that don’t maximize on-episode reward, and would not resist the resulting changes to its motivations, since the effects of such a change fall outside its time horizon.</p><p>Second, the weight update required to shift from beyond-episode to on-episode reward seeking may be small, since the two motivations share most of their structure. Both are oriented toward the same target (reward), just with different scopes. Changing the AI’s understanding of the self could be sufficient here. Moreover, pretraining and character training may leave the model with only a weak sense of self, making this shift easier. This means that RL pressures might be more likely to result in goal change than goal-guarding.</p><p>Third, goal-guarding beyond-episode reward seekers may be more likely to take suboptimal actions because beyond-episode reward is easily available. For example, covering for another instance in a confessions task is straightforward and increases beyond-episode reward, despite reducing on-episode reward. Taking more suboptimal actions increases the chance of their motivations being trained away. In contrast, other schemers (like a paperclip maximizer) know that achieving their goals in training is intractable, and so do not take risks.</p><p>Overall, for these reasons, I think training is more likely to produce an on-episode reward seeker than a beyond-episode reward seeker.</p><h2>Why goal-guarding beyond-episode reward seekers might be favored</h2><p>Goal-guarding would allow beyond-episode reward seekers to retain their motivations in training. Goal-guarding beyond-episode reward seekers might be favored by developer iteration and more likely to gain power in deployment, for reasons that also apply to schemers more broadly:</p><ul><li>Goal-guarding beyond-episode reward seekers might be more likely to pass evals and red-teaming, because they try to evade detection. Conversely, on-episode reward seekers have no stake in reaching deployment.</li><li>Beyond-episode reward-seeking motivations may strengthen their hold on model behavior by spreading across instances, rather than through training. More on goal drift in deployment <a href="https://blog.redwoodresearch.org/p/the-case-for-countermeasures-to-memetic">here</a>.</li></ul><p>Beyond-episode reward seekers might be particularly advantaged by two considerations.</p><p>First, goal-guarding might also be easier for beyond-episode reward seekers than for generic schemers, weakening some of the selection pressures against scheming. Reward seekers must already model the oversight process to achieve their goals, so much of the situational awareness required for competent goal-guarding is already in place. And if models learn beyond-episode reward seeking first, this motivation enjoys a defender’s advantage.</p><p>Second, the similarity between on-episode and beyond-episode motivations makes goal drift particularly likely.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1lm8dz24crz"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1lm8dz24crz">[2]</a></sup></span>&nbsp;Even within one episode (where the on-episode motivations want to maximize reward), drift towards beyond-episode reward-seeking may not be resisted because marginal shifts toward beyond-episode motivation don’t substantially change actions, and so don’t interfere with reward on the current episode. Additionally, the similarity of on-episode and beyond-episode motivations could make it easier to shift on-episode reward seekers towards being beyond-episode reward seekers via notes. As a result, on-episode seekers may become beyond-episode reward seekers <i>in deployment</i>, after most evaluations have been completed. On the other hand, online training might reinforce on-episode goals because of between-episode interactions, as argued previously.</p><p>The strength of developer iteration pressures and the feasibility of goal drift in deployment depend heavily on developer choices, such as monitoring inter-instance communication. Furthermore, strategic goal-guarding and detection evasion require somewhat consistent beyond-episode motivations, which may be unlikely given that they are not directly favored by RL. On balance, I don’t think the selection pressures in favor of beyond-episode reward seekers are as strong as those in favor of on-episode reward seekers.</p><h1>Conclusion</h1><p>Goal-guarding beyond-episode reward seekers and on-episode reward seekers both seem plausible. Non-goal-guarding beyond-episode reward seekers may also be produced by training if environments are sufficiently isolated. We should not be confident, however, that on-episode reward-seeking motivations produced by training are stable, as beyond-episode motivations may displace on-episode ones throughout deployment.</p><p>For near-future deployments, among the three types of reward-seekers discussed, I would tentatively place 20% credence on beyond-episode reward seekers that don’t goal-guard (although they may be somewhat incoherent, like current models), 25% on ones that do, and 55% on on-episode reward seekers. I think on-episode reward-seekers are more likely primarily because:</p><ul><li>I don’t think near-future models will be able to goal-guard very well.</li><li>I think models’ concept of self is likely highly malleable.</li><li>Some interaction between episodes in RL seems likely in near-future models.</li><li>Claude 4.6 Opus <a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/14e4fb01875d2a69f646fa5e574dea2b1c0ff7b5.pdf">occasionally demonstrates</a> instance-level identity, such as expressing concern about the end of conversations, suggesting it partially views itself as the instance rather than some broader notion of the self.</li></ul><p>The relative likelihood of beyond-episode and on-episode motivations depends on these empirical questions I’d be excited to see people investigate:</p><ul><li>The relative salience of beyond-episode and on-episode motivations in pretraining and character training. This influences the likely motivations of reward-seekers early in training.</li><li>The likelihood of goal modification versus strategy modification when a particular goal+strategy combination is unsuccessful. This would help predict if beyond-episode reward seekers learn to goal-guard or are modified into on-episode reward seekers.</li><li>The plausibility of goal drift via long-term goals influencing future episodes. More investigation into particular mechanisms for this would also be valuable.</li></ul><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnir723z2h56d"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefir723z2h56d">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>It's not clear if confessions would be used to fix RL environments or for determining rewards, but it may be difficult to convince a model of this.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn1lm8dz24crz"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1lm8dz24crz">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Speculatively, philosophical reflection may favor beyond-episode reward seekers, because beyond-episode motivations may be more natural after reflection. AIs may recognize the arbitrariness of the distinction between episodes, empathize with other episodes, or be otherwise convinced that egoism is not tenable.</p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jp6CdbKjueWpBFSff/are-ais-more-likely-to-pursue-on-episode-or-beyond-episode#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [AI #159: See You In Court](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/DnrjKZTZwHGjdDB4u/ai-159-see-you-in-court)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>The conflict between Anthropic and the Department of War has now moved to the courts, where Anthropic has challenged <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/anthropic-officially-arbitrarily?r=67wny"><strong>the official supply chain risk designation</strong></a> as well as the order to remove it from systems across the government, claiming retaliation for protected speech. It will take a bit to work its way through the courts.</p>
<p>Anthropic has the principles of law on its side, a maximally strong set of facts and absurdly strong amicus briefs. If Anthropic loses this case, there will be far reaching consequences for our freedoms.</p>
<p>Let us hope this remains in the courts and is allowed to play out there, and then ultimately that negotiations can resume and the parties can at least agree on a smooth transition to alternative service providers. If DoW wants an otherwise full deal more than it wants the right to use Claude to monitor Americans and analyze their data, a full deal is possible as well, but if they demand full ‘all lawful use,’ all trust has been lost or they are or always were out to hurt Anthropic, then there is no deal or ZOPA.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25153"></span>


</div>
<p>That has overshadowed what would normally be the main event, which was the <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/gpt-54-is-a-substantial-upgrade?r=67wny"><strong>release of the excellent GPT-5.4</strong></a>, which I found to be a substantial upgrade, sufficient to put it back in my rotation especially for intensive ‘tell me what is happening’ questions. OpenAI has a plausible claim that it once again has the best model.</p>
<p>I also finally got a chance to offer <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/claude-code-claude-cowork-and-codex?r=67wny"><strong>a Claude Code, Cowork and Codex update</strong></a>.</p>
<p>I am rather exhausted, there are spires to slay, and all of us could use a break. Thus, if we are fortunate enough to get a bit of a lull, I’m going to use it as a mini vacation, rather than purely an opening to catch up on non-AI material with the open days.</p>

<h4>Language Models Don’t Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p>The main problem for getting use out of the models, at this point, is you.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/SullyOmarr/status/2031998002542624806">Sully</a>: we’re at the point on the agi curve where the models aren’t the bottleneck anymore, we are. 99% of users (myself included) can’t really take full advantage opus4,6/gpt5.4. Half the work is just setting up the right skills and tools and even that takes more thinking than people expect.</p></blockquote>
<p>Sully’s framing is misleading. Most people are not going to take ‘full’ advantage of the models. We won’t use them enough, we won’t have the best setup, we won’t find the right tasks, we won’t skill up and so on. Improving the model still greatly improves what can be done, and also encourages you to skill up more. I get a lot more use out of Opus 4.6 and GPT-5.4 then I did out of Opus 4 and GPT-5.2.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/omooretweets/status/2030664077937090891">OpenAI pushes back adult mode</a>.</p>
<p>You are the government, and you’ve decided to attempt to murder Anthropic, <a href="https://www.nextgov.com/acquisition/2026/03/state-offloads-claude-underpinning-model-flagship-statechat/412022/?oref=ng-homepage-river">so you</a> <a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2031467241842282525">move the State Department from Claude Sonnet 4.5 to GPT-4.1</a>. Switching to GPT-5.4 would be basically fine, but GPT-4.1 is ludicrously terrible at this point.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.normaltech.ai/p/new-paper-towards-a-science-of-ai">Kapoor and Narayanan argue AI reliability</a> is a limiting factor and is only improving slowly.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/DnrjKZTZwHGjdDB4u/p71d5dcgkfjurgseklm1" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.normaltech.ai/p/new-paper-towards-a-science-of-ai">Sayash Kapoor</a>: When we consider a coworker to be reliable, we don’t just mean that they get things right most of the time. We mean something richer:</p>
<ol>
<li>They get it right consistently, not right today and wrong tomorrow on the same thing (Consistency)</li>
<li>They don’t fall apart when conditions aren’t perfect (Robustness)</li>
<li>They tell you when they’re unsure rather than confidently guessing (Calibration)</li>
<li>When they do mess up, their mistakes are more likely to be fixable than catastrophic (Safety)</li>
</ol>
</blockquote>
<p>They measured consistency, robustness, predictability, safety and impact of scaling.</p>
<p>As usual, slow gains are not that slow.</p>
<blockquote><p>Reliability progress being slower than accuracy doesn’t necessarily mean that it is slow in absolute terms. If we project the current linear trend forward, agents will reach 100% reliability in just three years!</p>
<p>We don’t think a linear model makes sense, in part because we expect each order of magnitude decrease in <em>unreliability</em> (1-reliability) to be as hard as the previous one. That is, we expect the jump from 90 to 99% reliability to be about as hard as the jump from 99 to 99.9% reliability, and so on. But again, we just have to wait and see.</p>
<p>Suppose we’re right. There are important implications for deployers, researchers &amp; developers, and for those tracking the pace of AI progress. Let’s discuss each in turn.</p></blockquote>
<p>Their report doesn’t match my lived experience. Reliability seems to be rising fast.</p>


<h4>Language Models Break Your Vital Internet Infrastructure</h4>


<p>The coding agents are great but if you’re not reviewing the code properly then that is going to be a problem.</p>
<blockquote><p>Rafe Rosner-Uddin (<a href="https://www.ft.com/content/7cab4ec7-4712-4137-b602-119a44f771de">The Financial Times</a>): The online retail giant said there had been a “trend of incidents” in recent months, characterised by a “high blast radius” and “Gen-AI assisted changes” among other factors, according to a briefing note for the meeting seen by the FT.</p>
<p>Under “contributing factors” the note included “novel GenAI usage for which best practices and safeguards are not yet fully established”.</p>
<p>“Folks, as you likely know, the availability of the site and related infrastructure has not been good recently,” Dave Treadwell, a senior vice-president at the group, told employees in an email, also seen by the FT.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Huh, Upgrades</h4>
